# AYUSHMAN BHARAT PM-JAY — UTTAR PRADESH
# Demand-Supply Gap Analysis Report

**Prepared for:** State Health Agency, Uttar Pradesh
**Data Period:** 2023-02 to 2026-01
**Report Generated:** 08 April 2026

---

## 1. Executive Summary

**Executive Summary**

Uttar Pradesh’s AB PM-JAY network has broad geographic reach on paper but weak effective access in practice. Across **641 blocks in 75 districts**, the scheme covers **205,847 enrolled beneficiaries**, with **22,500 cases recorded between February 2023 and January 2026**. Yet actual use remains low: only **21,346 distinct beneficiaries** have used the scheme, a **state-wide utilization rate of 10.4%**—roughly **10 out of every 100 enrolled people**. Supply is concentrated unevenly. The state has **800 empanelled hospitals** with **85,017 inpatient beds**, but this capacity is not available where many families live. **206 blocks have no empanelled hospital at all**, and **212 blocks depend on just one hospital**, leaving large parts of the state exposed to service disruption, travel burden, and referral delays. This mismatch between enrolment and usable supply defines the central challenge of PM-JAY implementation in Uttar Pradesh.

The analysis identifies a small set of high-impact failures that affect the largest number of families and should be treated as immediate priorities:

- **75 blocks are high-enrolment but low-utilization**, covering **30,021 enrolled beneficiaries**—about **15 out of every 100 enrolled people in the state** live in blocks where coverage exists but use remains weak.  
- **206 blocks have zero empanelled hospitals**, and **212 blocks rely on a single hospital**; together, this means **418 blocks**—about **two-thirds of all blocks**—have either no local provider choice or no provider at all.  
- **Only 1.6% of cases are treated within the same district**, showing that families are overwhelmingly forced to travel outside their district for care.  
- **Obstetrics & Gynaecology is the largest specialty gap**, with **1,010 unmet cases**, making it the single biggest missed service area in the network.  
- There are **1,567 block × disease mismatches** where the needed infrastructure is absent, meaning demand is visible but the required service cannot be delivered locally.  
- **Card activation is 87.4%**, but **160 blocks show high drop-off**, so many enrolled families still cannot convert eligibility into treatment.  
- **620 blocks show seasonal surges**, with demand peaking in **August–September**, but supply planning does not match this pattern.  
- **23 hospitals were delisted**, including **4 that were the only provider in their block**, immediately cutting off access for affected populations.  
- The total estimated **revenue leakage from specialty gaps is INR 35.3 crore**, reflecting both lost treatment opportunity for patients and lost purchasing efficiency for the scheme.

For families, this means enrolment does not guarantee care. In many blocks, a PM-JAY card exists but no nearby hospital can actually provide the needed treatment. In practical terms, a family may be eligible, may have completed card activation, and may still need to travel across district lines for admission. This is especially serious for time-sensitive conditions and for women needing obstetric and gynaecological care. Where a block has only one empanelled hospital, any delisting, service suspension, or capacity constraint can shut access entirely. Where activation drop-off is high, families are lost before they even reach the hospital. The result is a system where financial protection is promised statewide, but real access depends heavily on where a family lives.

The report that follows is organized into five sections. **Section 1** maps enrolment, utilization, and hospital supply across districts and blocks to show where the demand-supply gap is largest. **Section 2** identifies high-enrolment, low-utilization blocks and names the populations most affected. **Section 3** examines specialty-level gaps, with particular focus on Obstetrics & Gynaecology and other high-unmet service areas. **Section 4** analyzes operational barriers—card activation drop-off, seasonal demand peaks, patient movement outside district, and the impact of hospital delisting. **Section 5** sets out an action agenda for the state: where to empanel first, where to restore provider continuity, where to target activation drives, and how to align supply with predictable demand so that PM-JAY coverage translates into actual treatment.

---

## 2. Where People Can't Access Care

## Where People Can't Access Care

### 2.1 High enrolment, low use: families are covered on paper but not getting treatment

- The biggest access gap is not enrolment alone; it is enrolment without use. In 75 blocks, many families are enrolled but very few actually use PM-JAY services.

- Statewide utilization is 10.4%, which means only about 10 out of 100 enrolled families use care. In these flagged blocks, use falls below that benchmark despite large enrolled populations.

- Mohanlalganj in Lucknow has 652 enrolled families but only 8.4% utilization, meaning only about 8 out of 100 families use care.

- Chinhat in Lucknow shows the same pattern: 599 enrolled families, but only 8.3% utilization, or just over 8 out of 100 families.

- Baragaon in Varanasi has 575 enrolled families and 8.3% utilization, showing weak conversion of coverage into treatment.

- Chiraigaon in Varanasi also has 556 enrolled families and 8.3% utilization, confirming a repeated access problem within the district.

- Sarbanandapur in Kanpur Nagar has 554 enrolled families but only 8.1% utilization, meaning roughly 92 out of 100 enrolled families do not use care.

- Lakhimpur Block 1 in Lakhimpur Kheri is among the weakest large blocks: 516 enrolled families and only 7.2% utilization, or about 7 out of 100 families.

- Bahraich Block 7 in Bahraich has 514 enrolled families and 7.6% utilization, leaving more than 9 in 10 enrolled families without recorded use.

- Bulandshahr Block 3 has 513 enrolled families and 9.0% utilization, still below the state average despite a large enrolled base.

- Moradabad Block 7 has 512 enrolled families and 8.8% utilization, showing that access remains weak even in a major urban district.

- Bijnor Block 3 has 505 enrolled families and 8.7% utilization, again below the state average and below what enrolment levels should deliver.

- Lucknow appears twice in the top 10 problem blocks through Mohanlalganj and Chinhat, showing that access gaps persist even near a major referral hub.

- Varanasi also appears twice in the top 10 through Baragaon and Chiraigaon, pointing to weak local service use around a major medical center.

- District clustering is strong. Gonda has 4 high-enrolment, low-use blocks, the highest count in the state.

- Moradabad also has 4 such blocks, showing repeated block-level access failure within the same district.

- Bulandshahr, Rampur, and Pratapgarh each have 3 problem blocks, which means the issue is district-wide, not isolated to one location.

- These blocks represent missed access, not low demand. Families are enrolled, but treatment is not happening at expected levels.

- In practical terms, these are places where the card exists but care does not. The gap is between entitlement and actual hospital use.

### 2.2 No nearby provider: zero-hospital and single-hospital blocks

- The sharpest supply gap is complete absence of empanelled hospitals. Uttar Pradesh has 206 blocks with zero hospitals under PM-JAY.

- These zero-supply blocks cover 58,417 enrolled beneficiaries, meaning tens of thousands of people hold coverage but have no local empanelled hospital option.

- Kakori in Lucknow is the largest zero-supply block, with 746 enrolled beneficiaries and no empanelled hospital in the block.

- Jaunpur Block 4 has 585 enrolled beneficiaries and zero hospitals, leaving a large covered population without local access.

- Jaunpur Block 3 has 570 enrolled beneficiaries and zero hospitals, showing a repeated supply gap within the same district.

- Rohania in Varanasi has 559 enrolled beneficiaries and no empanelled hospital, despite being in a district with major tertiary care.

- Mehnagar in Azamgarh has 538 enrolled beneficiaries and zero hospitals, forcing patients to leave the block for treatment.

- Jaunpur stands out because two of the top five zero-supply blocks are in the district, matching its later pattern of patient outflow.

- Lucknow and Varanasi also appear in the top zero-supply list, showing that district-level hospital presence does not guarantee block-level access.

- Beyond zero supply, 212 blocks have only one empanelled hospital. These single-hospital blocks cover 66,952 enrolled beneficiaries.

- A single-hospital block means no real choice for patients. If that one provider is inactive, overloaded, or selective, access effectively collapses.

- Four blocks have already seen this risk materialize because their sole provider was delisted.

- In Gonda Block 6, the only listed provider was Primary Health Centre Gonda Block 6; after delisting, the block lost its sole local option.

- In Farrukhabad Block 8, Women Hospital Farrukhabad was the only provider; delisting removed the block’s only empanelled hospital.

- In Bilhaur, Kanpur Nagar, Dr. Verma Nursing Home was the sole provider; delisting left the block without that single access point.

- In Gonda Block 7, Agarwal & Tiwari Nursing Home was the only provider; delisting created another direct supply shock in Gonda.

- Gonda is especially exposed because it has two sole-provider delistings and also the highest number of high-enrolment, low-use blocks.

- The combined picture is clear: many blocks have no provider at all, and many more depend on just one hospital that can disappear overnight.

- For families in these blocks, the access problem starts before treatment decisions. There is simply no nearby PM-JAY facility to use.

### 2.3 Patients are traveling out: local systems are not retaining care

- Local retention is extremely low. Only 1.6% of cases are treated within the same block and district.

- This means fewer than 2 out of 100 patients get care close to home. More than 98 out of 100 must leave their local area for treatment.

- Ten blocks recorded zero local retention, meaning not a single patient was treated within the same block and district.

- Akola in Agra had 22 cases and 0% retention, so every patient left the local area for care.

- Ambedkar Block 1 had 35 cases and 0% retention; Ambedkar Blocks 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 also had 0% retention.

- In Ambedkar Nagar, this means six separate blocks sent every recorded patient outside the local area for treatment.

- Amethi Block 3 had 26 cases and 0% retention; Amethi Blocks 4 and 5 also had 0% retention.

- In Amethi, three blocks retained none of their patients locally, showing a complete absence of effective nearby treatment pathways.

- Cross-district travel is also concentrated in a few strong pull centers. Prayagraj sends 26 cases each to Bareilly, Gorakhpur, and Hardoi.

- Prayagraj also sends 23 cases to Aligarh and 22 cases each to Kanpur Nagar, Lucknow, and Varanasi.

- Aligarh sends 22 cases to Gorakhpur, and Jaunpur sends 22 cases to Gorakhpur, showing long-distance movement toward specific treatment hubs.

- Agra sends 21 cases to Prayagraj, confirming that patient movement is not just local spillover but inter-district dependence.

- Jaunpur is the largest net exporter, with a net outflow of 198 patients. This matches its zero-supply blocks and weak local access.

- Bahraich has a net outflow of 156 patients, showing that many residents must leave the district to get PM-JAY care.

- Ghaziabad has a net outflow of 118 patients, proving that even high-capacity districts can have block-level or network-level access gaps.

- Lakhimpur Kheri has a net outflow of 117 patients, consistent with low utilization in Lakhimpur Block 1.

- Gonda has a net outflow of 115 patients, which aligns with its multiple low-use blocks and sole-provider delisting shocks.

- Gorakhpur is the largest net importer, receiving 316 more patients than it sends out. It is functioning as a major treatment destination.

- Lucknow is the second-largest net importer with 171, confirming its role as a referral hub for surrounding districts.

- Hardoi receives a net 152 patients, Hathras 147, and Gautam Buddha Nagar 131, showing where patients are finding available care.

- The access gap is therefore geographic as well as institutional: patients are not just bypassing one hospital, they are bypassing entire blocks and districts.

- Where local retention is zero, the PM-JAY promise depends on travel, time, and out-of-pocket spending outside the package, even if treatment itself is covered.

- The biggest access gaps are concentrated in blocks with enrolled families but low use, blocks with no hospital or only one hospital, and districts that consistently lose patients to outside providers.

- The most urgent locations are Kakori in Lucknow; Jaunpur Blocks 3 and 4; Rohania in Varanasi; Mehnagar in Azamgarh; Mohanlalganj and Chinhat in Lucknow; Baragaon and Chiraigaon in Varanasi; Lakhimpur Block 1; Bahraich Block 7; and multiple blocks in Gonda, Moradabad, Bulandshahr, Rampur, Pratapgarh, Ambedkar Nagar, and Amethi.

## 3. What's Missing Where



---

## 4. Administrative Barriers

## Administrative Barriers

- Administrative gaps are slowing scheme use even where hospitals exist: card activation remains incomplete, many families drop out before approval, and repeat visits add friction for patients.
- State-wide, card activation is 87.4%, which means about 87 out of 100 eligible families get an active card, but 13 out of 100 still do not complete the process.
- The problem is broad, not isolated: 160 blocks show high card drop-off and another 160 blocks show high rejection in the approval process.
- These are process failures inside the scheme pathway, not hospital-capacity issues; families are getting stuck before they can use PM-JAY benefits.

### 4.1 Card drop-off

- Card drop-off is the clearest administrative barrier: families start the process but do not reach activation, leaving entitlement unused.
- In Sambhal Block 6, Sambhal, activation is 78.7%, so 21 out of 100 families drop off before completion.
- In Chhata, Mathura, activation is 80.4%, meaning nearly 20 out of 100 families do not complete card activation.
- In Ambedkar Block 4, Ambedkar Nagar, activation is 80.7%, so 19 out of 100 families fall out of the process.
- In Sant Block 3, Sant Kabir Nagar, activation is 81.5%, which means about 18 out of 100 families drop off.
- In Chandauli Block 2, Chandauli, activation is 81.7%, so 18 out of 100 families do not reach an active card.
- In Sant Block 2, Sant Ravidas Nagar, activation is 81.8%, meaning just over 18 out of 100 families drop off.
- In Faridpur, Bareilly, activation is 81.8%, so about 18 out of 100 families fail to complete activation.
- In Shamli Block 4, Shamli, activation is 82.3%, meaning nearly 18 out of 100 families drop out.
- In Hapur Block 5, Hapur, activation is 82.8%, so 17 out of 100 families do not complete the process.
- In Sant Block 1, Sant Ravidas Nagar, activation is also 82.8%, meaning 17 out of 100 families drop off before card activation.
- These top-drop-off blocks are spread across western, central, and eastern Uttar Pradesh, showing a state-wide process problem rather than a single-district exception.
- The practical effect is direct: eligible families remain uninsured on paper at the point of need, even though they are already identified in the scheme database.
- High rejection in 160 blocks points to a second administrative choke point after enrolment start, indicating document, verification, or approval-stage failures.
- Together, drop-off and rejection reduce the usable PM-JAY pool before any hospital claim is raised, weakening scheme reach in high-need blocks.

### 4.2 Repeat visits

- State-wide, the repeat visit rate is 5.2%, which means about 5 out of 100 PM-JAY cases require another visit after the first contact.
- A total of 155 blocks show high repeat rates, indicating recurring process burden for patients and hospitals across the state.
- No block is flagged for possible treatment failure, so repeat visits are not being driven by poor clinical outcomes in the available data.
- This points to administrative causes such as incomplete paperwork, delayed approvals, follow-up verification, or claim-related return visits.
- For poor households, even one extra visit means added travel cost, wage loss, and delay in treatment despite nominal cashless coverage.
- The pattern matters because repeat visits reduce effective access: a family may be technically covered but still face multiple trips before care is completed.
- With 155 blocks showing high repeat rates and zero treatment-failure flags, the priority is to simplify scheme steps rather than expand clinical services alone.
- Administrative streamlining should focus first on blocks with both high card drop-off and high repeat burden, where families face barriers before and during care access.

---

## 5. Seasonal Patterns

### Main finding

- Demand surges are predictable in much of Uttar Pradesh: 620 blocks show clear seasonal spikes in PM-JAY use.
- The surge window is concentrated in the monsoon period, with August peaking in 273 blocks, July in 247 blocks, and September in 233 blocks.
- In plain terms, many blocks face their highest claim pressure in 3 consecutive months, so hospitals and district teams can prepare in advance.
- Most seasonal spikes are not tied to one named disease group in the data: 598 of 620 seasonal blocks are marked as “OTHER,” showing broad service pressure rather than a single-condition outbreak.

### When surges happen

- August is the single most common peak month, affecting 273 blocks, followed closely by July and September.
- This means the main statewide demand surge falls in the July-September period, aligned with the monsoon and immediate post-monsoon months.
- The pattern is strong enough to support pre-positioning of beds, staff, diagnostics, and referral transport before July each year.
- Outside the monsoon peak, a smaller set of blocks shows local surge months in winter or spring, so district planning must also track block-specific calendars.

### Where volatility is highest

- Auraiya Block 4 in Auraiya is the most volatile block, with a seasonality index of 2.31 and peaks in January, February, and April; the main surge disease is communicable conditions.
- Kasganj Block 8 in Kasganj is the second most volatile, with a seasonality index of 2.11 and peaks in April and December; the main surge disease is NCDs.
- Kasganj Block 7 in Kasganj ranks third, with a seasonality index of 1.89 and peaks in October and November; the main surge disease is communicable conditions.
- Banda Block 6 in Banda shows sharp swings, with a seasonality index of 1.78 and peaks in January, April, and September; the main surge disease is communicable conditions.
- Mahoba Block 5 in Mahoba is also highly seasonal, with a seasonality index of 1.77 and peaks in March and August; the main surge disease is classified as OTHER.

### What this means for predictability

- Seasonal demand surges are predictable at the state level because the highest-pressure months are clustered, not random.
- Seasonal demand is also predictable at the block level, but the exact peak month differs across blocks such as Auraiya, Kasganj, Banda, and Mahoba.
- Districts with highly volatile blocks should not rely only on state averages; they need block-wise monthly readiness plans.
- The data supports a fixed annual preparedness cycle: review claims by block in May-June, strengthen empanelled capacity before July, and maintain surge readiness through September.

### Operational implication

- UP should treat July-September as the core annual surge season for PM-JAY demand across a large share of blocks.
- Districts with outlier patterns, especially Auraiya, Kasganj, Banda, and Mahoba, need separate local calendars for staffing and referral management.
- Because 598 seasonal blocks are driven by “OTHER” conditions, planning should focus on total hospital load, not only one disease program.
- Seasonal spikes are foreseeable enough to guide contracting, bed planning, and fund flow timing, reducing avoidable service gaps during peak months.
